The chapter uses this to show how self-examination becomes more useful when it focuses on a real, persistent weakness rather than on general declarations of virtue.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a situation in which another person’s success produced resentment and examine what comparison or unmet expectation was involved."
Key point:
- Envy turns another person’s good fortune into a perceived threat.
- Naming a recurring vice is a necessary step toward managing it.
27. Q: What strategy did Johnson use when he felt envy, and what limitations does the chapter reveal in that strategy?
A: Show answer
Johnson sometimes countered envy by appealing to pride: he told himself that envying another person admitted inferiority and that he should instead recognize his own merits. He acknowledged that one vice should not normally be cured by another, but considered this tactic preferable to allowing envy to dominate. The strategy may interrupt resentment in the moment, yet it risks replacing one form of self-centered comparison with another. The chapter also shows Johnson moving toward charity and mercy, which offer a more generous way to view other people’s hidden troubles.
Study instruction:
- "Compare Johnson’s pride-based response to envy with a response based on gratitude, charity, and attention to the other person’s good."
Key point:
- Johnson used a partial remedy to interrupt a damaging emotion.
- A stronger long-term response shifts attention away from competitive self-evaluation.
28. Q: How did Johnson use charity and mercy to weaken envy?
A: Show answer
Johnson reasoned that other people’s lives contain hidden troubles and that apparent success rarely produces complete satisfaction. If everyone carries burdens, there are few people who should be envied as though they possess an uncomplicated happiness. This insight encouraged charity: instead of resenting another person’s advantage, he could recognize their vulnerability and wish them well. Mercy did not require denying their achievements or pretending that inequality was unreal. It changed the moral lens through which he interpreted them. The chapter presents compassion as a more humane response than constant comparison.
Study instruction:
- "When you feel envy, identify one difficulty the other person may face and one reason their success need not diminish your own worth."
Key point:
- Compassion can interrupt the fantasy that other people have effortless lives.
- Mercy redirects attention from rivalry toward shared human vulnerability.
29. Q: Why does Johnson describe sorrow as different from passions such as hunger or fear?
A: Show answer
Johnson observes that some passions naturally move toward their own resolution: hunger can lead to eating, and fear can lead to escape. Sorrow is different because it can remain fixed on the past and on what cannot be restored. It repeatedly wishes that events had been otherwise, which can make it intensify rather than resolve itself. The chapter uses this analysis to show why sorrow may require deliberate intervention. A person cannot always reason grief away, but can introduce movement, new ideas, and meaningful activity that prevent life from becoming stagnant.
Study instruction:
- "Describe why regret about an unchangeable past can persist even when the original event is no longer occurring."
Key point:
- Sorrow can reinforce itself by repeatedly returning attention to an irreversible loss.
- Understanding the structure of an emotion helps identify a practical response.
30. Q: What does Johnson mean by calling employment a general antidote against sorrow?
A: Show answer
Johnson recommends useful activity because it introduces movement, new ideas, and engagement with the world. Employment can interrupt the stagnation in which sorrow repeatedly circles around the past. The chapter does not suggest that work erases grief or that a grieving person should be denied time to mourn. It presents purposeful activity as one support that can prevent sorrow from consuming every part of life. The most constructive employment is meaningful and proportionate, allowing a person to remain connected to responsibilities and other people while acknowledging the loss.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one manageable task that could restore routine and connection during a difficult period without treating work as a substitute for grieving."
Key point:
- Purposeful activity can interrupt stagnant rumination.
- Employment supports recovery but does not make grief disappear automatically.
31. Q: How did Johnson use essays as exercises in self-confrontation?
A: Show answer
Johnson wrote about the very subjects that troubled him, including despair, pride, boredom, gluttony, guilt, vanity, and envy. The essays were not simply lectures to other people; they were a way to bring his own problems into view and examine them in language. By describing a weakness, he could identify its structure, consequences, and possible remedies. The chapter emphasizes that he did not believe he could lecture himself into perfect virtue. Writing instead helped him plan, observe, and train his will with greater honesty.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a recurring personal difficulty and outline an essay that explains its triggers, consequences, and realistic responses."
Key point:
- Writing can make a vague struggle specific enough to examine.
- Self-confrontation is more effective when it produces practical insight rather than self-condemnation.
32. Q: What does Johnson mean by the stability of truth, and how did it help him form convictions?
A: Show answer
Johnson sought stability by disciplining himself to see things as they were rather than allowing vanity, fear, or fantasy to determine his view. He built convictions through observation, reading, moral reflection, and attention to particular people. His approach did not depend on a single abstract system that explained everything. The chapter presents truth as an anchor amid emotional and intellectual change. A person may remain imperfect and uncertain in many areas, yet become more dependable by learning to test impressions and resist convenient distortions.
Study instruction:
- "Take a strong opinion and list the observations or evidence that support it, then identify what might disconfirm it."
Key point:
- Truth provides a stable reference point amid changing moods and circumstances.
- Conviction is stronger when it has been tested against reality.
33. Q: How does Michel de Montaigne provide a contrasting model of self-examination to Johnson?
A: Show answer
Johnson pursued self-formation through strenuous moral effort, self-criticism, and confrontation with weakness. Montaigne approached himself with more irony, curiosity, and acceptance. He sought to understand his changing nature by observing his own thoughts and reactions, then writing essays that allowed a coherent perspective to emerge. The contrast is not between seriousness and carelessness: both men were deeply concerned with virtue and self-knowledge. They differed in temperament and method. Johnson tried to discipline himself toward an ideal; Montaigne tried to understand and live wisely within his actual nature.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the two methods and identify which circumstances might make one more useful than the other."
Key point:
- Johnson and Montaigne pursued self-knowledge through different temperaments and practices.
- There is more than one serious route to character formation.
34. Q: How did Montaigne’s upbringing differ from Johnson’s, and why does that context matter?
A: Show answer
Montaigne grew up in a wealthy, established family and received a gentle humanist education designed to make him well-rounded. Johnson experienced illness, poverty, social disadvantage, and harsh schooling. Their different circumstances influenced the conditions under which they examined themselves. Montaigne later retired to the relative security of his estate, while Johnson worked amid the competitive, precarious life of London. The chapter cautions against treating either man’s method as a universal recipe without considering temperament, resources, and historical context.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the material and social conditions that supported Montaigne’s reflective life with the pressures shaping Johnson’s work and self-discipline."
Key point:
- Character practices develop within particular life circumstances.
- A method that suits one person may not transfer unchanged to another.
35. Q: Why did Montaigne begin writing essays about himself?
A: Show answer
After retiring from public life, Montaigne expected contemplation to bring tranquillity but discovered that his mind remained restless and fragmented. Writing about his own changing thoughts became a way to study that movement rather than pretend it did not exist. His essays became an experiment in self-understanding and a method of bringing some order to a fluid inner life. He also believed that careful self-observation could reveal features of human nature more broadly. His project was not merely to publicize private details; it was to understand how people think, feel, change, and fail.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how describing changing thoughts can help a person recognize patterns that would be missed by a single fixed self-description."
Key point:
- Montaigne used writing to investigate the changing self.
- Honest self-description can reveal common features of human experience.
36. Q: What does Montaigne’s image of the restless mind suggest about the limits of self-control?
A: Show answer
Montaigne found that thoughts moved unpredictably from one perception to another and that neither mind nor body always obeyed conscious intention. He used this experience to question the fantasy that people can fully command themselves. The chapter presents his writing as a way to observe this instability without pretending to eliminate it. Recognizing limits does not mean giving up responsibility. It means making judgments that account for the changing and imperfect nature of the person who must act.
Study instruction:
- "Record a series of thoughts during a short period and note how attention shifts without deliberate choice."
Key point:
- The mind is not always as orderly or obedient as people imagine.
- Realistic self-understanding begins by acknowledging limits of control.
37. Q: How did Montaigne’s essays create a form of self-integration?
A: Show answer
Montaigne’s essays gathered observations that might otherwise have remained scattered and temporary. Their informal structure allowed related thoughts to accumulate over time, producing a recognizable perspective without forcing every experience into a rigid system. Writing helped him see patterns in his reactions and develop a more stable relationship with his changing nature. The chapter describes this as an act of integration: he did not eliminate variation but learned to understand it and live with it. His prose style became an expression of the balance he was trying to cultivate.
Study instruction:
- "Choose several notes on one recurring issue and organize them into a coherent reflection without removing genuine contradictions."
Key point:
- Integration can come from understanding variation rather than suppressing it.
- A flexible written record can help organize an unsettled inner life.
38. Q: What makes Montaigne’s self-revelation morally demanding rather than merely confessional?
A: Show answer
Montaigne aimed to describe his weaknesses and contradictions honestly, even when the result was unflattering. The chapter emphasizes that his purpose was not fame, attention, or the display of private experience. He wanted self-knowledge that could support a coherent and disciplined life. This required facing truths that public performance often conceals. In private, where applause and reputation are absent, a person must judge whether their conduct matches their stated values. The moral demand lies in candor and the willingness to revise self-understanding.
Study instruction:
- "Write a private account of a weakness without trying to make yourself look better or worse than the evidence warrants."
Key point:
- Honest self-revelation is a form of moral work.
- The aim is self-respect grounded in truth, not public admiration.
39. Q: What does Montaigne mean by disciplining oneself “within,” and why does he regard it as difficult?
A: Show answer
Montaigne argues that performing an honest role in public is easier than governing oneself when no audience is present. Private discipline requires confronting motives that can be hidden, rationalized, or indulged without external correction. It is difficult because the person is both the observer and the subject of examination, and can easily protect their preferred image. The chapter presents this inward discipline as a demanding alternative to public displays of virtue. It asks whether a person can act with integrity when no reputation is at stake.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a private behavior that would remain the same even if nobody could reward or criticize you for it."
Key point:
- Integrity is tested most clearly when public approval is absent.
- Private self-governance is harder than performing an admirable public role.
40. Q: How does Montaigne’s humble but secure view of himself differ from self-contempt?
A: Show answer
Montaigne openly acknowledged limitations in memory, physical appearance, skill, and temperament, but did not treat these weaknesses as proof that he was worthless. His self-assessment was low in the sense that it resisted grandiosity, yet secure enough to admit shortcomings without panic. The chapter presents this as a form of equipoise: he could observe a flaw, sometimes with humor, and continue living. This differs from self-contempt, which turns a weakness into a total judgment of the person. Honest humility recognizes limitations while preserving proportion and dignity.
Study instruction:
- "Write one accurate statement about a personal limitation and one about a genuine strength without exaggerating either."
Key point:
- Humility is compatible with a stable sense of worth.
- Acknowledging weakness need not become self-condemnation.
41. Q: What role did wonder and astonishment play in Montaigne’s search for equipoise?
A: Show answer
Montaigne’s account of his near-fatal riding accident showed him how different outward appearances can be from inner experience. What looked terrifying to observers felt peaceful and strangely sweet to him as consciousness returned. The episode reinforced his sense of how mysterious and unpredictable life is. Wonder helped loosen rigid expectations and encouraged a calmer response to events. The chapter links this capacity for astonishment with his ability to maintain an even keel, neither becoming excessively elated by success nor overwhelmed by setbacks.
Study instruction:
- "Describe an experience that challenged your assumptions about what another person was feeling or experiencing."
Key point:
- Wonder can interrupt overconfidence about what an experience means.
- Astonishment encourages humility and a more flexible view of life.
42. Q: How did Montaigne turn personal limitations into compensating practices?
A: Show answer
Montaigne tried to adapt his habits to the person he actually was. Because he read slowly, he focused on fewer books. Because his mind wandered, he used the movement of thought to explore multiple perspectives. Because he was not naturally severe, he favored moderation and relaxation over constant self-reproach. The chapter does not suggest that every weakness automatically becomes a strength. It shows how realistic self-knowledge can support practical adjustments that make a person more effective and less consumed by self-criticism.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one limitation and identify a practical adjustment that would reduce its costs without pretending it does not exist."
Key point:
- Self-knowledge can help a person design around limitations.
- Adaptation is more useful than either denial or self-punishment.
43. Q: What are the risks of Montaigne’s self-acceptance, and how does the chapter acknowledge them?
A: Show answer
Critics may see Montaigne’s moderation as emotional distance, conflict avoidance, or self-satisfaction. He himself admitted that he sometimes escaped painful thoughts by changing direction rather than confronting them. The chapter recognizes that this strategy can become evasive if it prevents a person from addressing a real responsibility or injustice. Self-acceptance is not automatically wise; it must be tested against the demands of relationships and the wider world. Montaigne’s strengths include humility and perspective, but his approach also has limitations that readers should examine honestly.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish a wise decision to stop unproductive rumination from an avoidant decision to evade a necessary conversation or duty."
Key point:
- Self-acceptance can become avoidance if it replaces responsibility.
- A balanced approach combines perspective with willingness to face difficult realities.
44. Q: Why was friendship central to Montaigne’s vision of a good society?
A: Show answer
Montaigne’s essay on friendship celebrates his close relationship with Étienne de la Boétie, whose death left a lasting absence. He describes friendship as a profound union of trust, thought, affection, and shared life. The chapter presents this bond as the highest form of social connection in Montaigne’s vision, rather than a pursuit of status or abstract perfection. Friendship matters because it allows people to know and support one another deeply. It is not merely social convenience; it is a relationship in which each person can entrust themselves to the other.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the qualities that distinguish deep friendship from networking, convenience, or mutual advantage."
Key point:
- Friendship is central to Montaigne’s account of a humane society.
- Trust and shared concern give relationships depth beyond utility.
45. Q: How do Johnson and Montaigne differ in the way they approach moral struggle?
A: Show answer
Johnson is intense, self-demanding, and combative. He seeks to reform himself through effort, confrontation, disciplined work, and moral criticism. Montaigne is more moderate and amused by human weakness, using candid observation and self-acceptance to cultivate balance. Both are serious about virtue and both use literature to create order from the confusion of life. The contrast is not a simple choice between effort and laziness. It is a comparison of methods shaped by different temperaments, resources, and historical pressures.
Study instruction:
- "Create a two-column comparison of Johnson and Montaigne covering temperament, method, emotional style, writing, and view of weakness."
Key point:
- Both men pursued moral understanding through different methods.
- The best lesson may be to learn from each approach according to the situation.
46. Q: Why does the chapter suggest that Johnson’s harder method may suit some people better than Montaigne’s gentler approach?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s author judges that Johnson’s character was shaped through sustained confrontation with difficult events and deliberate effort. Montaigne’s moderation was supported partly by wealth, security, and the ability to retire from public conflict. A person facing demanding responsibilities may need structure, effort, and direct correction rather than relying on gentle observation alone. At the same time, Johnson’s severity can become exhausting if copied without regard to individual temperament. The chapter invites readers to consider which method fits their circumstances rather than turning either man into a universal template.
Study instruction:
- "Assess your current circumstances and decide whether you need firmer discipline, greater self-acceptance, or a combination of both."
Key point:
- Character formation is affected by both method and life circumstances.
- A useful approach must fit the person and the demands of the situation.
47. Q: How did Johnson’s work on the dictionary reflect his desire to impose order on language and life?
A: Show answer
Johnson’s dictionary project required years of reading, collecting quotations, defining words, and organizing evidence. The work was tedious, but he valued its usefulness and relative safety from vanity and public contention. The chapter connects this external task with his effort to bring order to his own inner life. The dictionary gave structure to his time and directed his abilities toward a public good. It shows how disciplined, ordinary work can support character when it is connected to service and sustained attention rather than only to prestige.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a demanding project whose repetitive tasks could create structure and serve a purpose beyond personal recognition."
Key point:
- Useful work can organize attention and contribute to a wider good.
- Orderly practice can support both public service and personal discipline.
48. Q: How did Tetty’s death affect Johnson’s moral and emotional life?
A: Show answer
Johnson was deeply affected by the death of his wife, Elizabeth Porter, whom he called Tetty. His journals record grief, prayer, and repeated promises to reform himself in her memory. The chapter shows that his emotional life remained intense even as he developed a more coherent public and intellectual identity. Grief did not disappear into productivity; it became part of his continuing moral reflection. His response illustrates how love and loss can renew commitments while also exposing unresolved guilt and sorrow.
Study instruction:
- "Examine how a significant loss can change a person’s priorities and the promises they make to themselves."
Key point:
- Tetty’s death remained an important part of Johnson’s inner life.
- Grief can deepen reflection without being reduced to a productivity problem.
49. Q: How did Johnson’s treatment of marginalized people demonstrate his moral commitments?
A: Show answer
Johnson’s home included people who were poor, disabled, formerly enslaved, or otherwise socially marginalized. He sometimes took people in despite the conflict and inconvenience this created. The chapter presents these actions as evidence that his moral concern was not confined to writing or conversation. He also helped friends by producing work for them without payment, using his abilities to protect their dignity or support their opportunities. His generosity was practical and sometimes disorderly, but it showed a willingness to accept burdens for the sake of others.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a concrete form of assistance that uses your skills or resources to protect another person’s dignity."
Key point:
- Johnson’s compassion was expressed through practical hospitality and service.
- Moral commitments become visible in the burdens a person is willing to share.
50. Q: Why did Johnson write substantial work for friends without receiving payment?
A: Show answer
Johnson used his writing ability to help friends whose circumstances or abilities limited their opportunities. The chapter describes him preparing a book for an elderly former physician and writing extensive law lectures for a friend who was not a strong writer. These efforts were not ordinary commercial transactions. They were acts of generosity intended to preserve another person’s work, reputation, or sense of contribution. The examples reveal that Johnson’s practical moral life included using his talent for others, even though he also valued earning money from his profession.
Study instruction:
- "Consider how a professional skill could be used to help someone whose work or contribution might otherwise be overlooked."
Key point:
- Talent can serve others as well as support personal livelihood.
- Generosity sometimes means giving expertise without seeking recognition.
51. Q: How did Johnson redirect his rebellious temperament toward self-combat?
A: Show answer
Johnson had a strong instinct to resist authority, visible from childhood through his university years and adult life. The chapter describes him turning that rebellious energy inward, using it to confront vanity, laziness, envy, and self-deception. Instead of directing every challenge outward, he treated his own weaknesses as worthy opponents. This did not make him gentle in every interaction, but it gave his forceful nature a moral purpose. The example shows that a powerful trait can be redirected rather than simply suppressed.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a strong personal tendency and consider how it could be redirected toward a constructive internal or social purpose."
Key point:
- Johnson converted rebellious energy into a form of moral self-discipline.
- Character development can involve redirecting a trait rather than eliminating it.
52. Q: What did Johnson’s visit to Uttoxeter reveal about repentance and atonement?
A: Show answer
As an elderly man, Johnson returned to the market square where he had refused his father’s request to help at the family bookstall. He understood pride to have been the source of his earlier refusal and felt lasting shame. Standing bareheaded in the rain was his attempt to acknowledge and atone for the wrong. The chapter presents this act as an unusually concrete expression of moral memory. Repentance involved more than admitting that he had been wrong; it required a symbolic action that brought him back to the place associated with the failure.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a past wrong that still matters and identify whether an apology, restitution, or other concrete act of repair is appropriate."
Key point:
- Repentance can require more than private regret.
- Atonement seeks to acknowledge harm and restore moral responsibility.
53. Q: How did Johnson’s fear of damnation shape his final years and his attitude toward death?
A: Show answer
Johnson remained deeply concerned about divine judgment and the possibility of eternal punishment. The chapter describes his watch inscription reminding him that night would come and his fear that he might be damned. In his final illness he resisted sedation because he wanted to remain conscious before God, and he endured severe pain while trying to prolong life. His response reveals the intensity of his religious conscience, but also the burden of a fear that never fully disappeared. The chapter presents this as part of his character rather than as a simple triumph of serenity.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish religious seriousness from fear-driven anxiety when reviewing Johnson’s final statements and actions."
Key point:
- Johnson’s moral life remained marked by fear as well as conviction.
- His final struggle shows that integration does not necessarily remove deep anxiety.
54. Q: What does the chapter mean when it describes Johnson as an example of humane wisdom?
A: Show answer
Johnson’s many abilities eventually formed a distinctive way of seeing and judging the world. His journalism, biography, theology, conversation, and practical advice became difficult to separate because each expressed a common concern for human motives and moral conduct. His wisdom was humane because it combined intellectual clarity with sympathy for weakness and suffering. He had not eliminated his contradictions, but he had learned to understand them and to respond to others with greater insight. The chapter presents this as a substantial achievement built through a lifetime of mental labor.
Study instruction:
- "Identify how intellectual clarity and compassion can reinforce each other in a difficult judgment about another person."
Key point:
- Humane wisdom combines accurate observation with sympathy.
- A coherent moral outlook can emerge through sustained work rather than effortless talent.
55. Q: Why does the chapter connect Johnson’s honesty with courage?
A: Show answer
Johnson was willing to approach fears, anxieties, and weaknesses directly rather than allowing them to remain vague and intimidating. The chapter describes this as a courage of honesty: looking closely at what is actually present, even when the truth is uncomfortable. By naming a fear or vanity, he could sometimes expose how exaggerated or absurd it had become. Honesty did not guarantee an easy solution, but it reduced the power of self-deception. The chapter also associates this courage with Montaigne, whose candid self-description similarly required a willingness to face unflattering facts.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one fear or rationalization and describe it in plain factual language without exaggeration or excuse."
Key point:
- Honesty can reduce the power of imagined threats and self-deception.
- Courage includes the willingness to see oneself accurately.
56. Q: What final lesson do Johnson and Montaigne offer about self-examination and the formation of character?
A: Show answer
Johnson and Montaigne show that a person can build greater coherence through different forms of honest self-study. Johnson emphasizes moral effort, useful work, confrontation, and disciplined truth-telling. Montaigne emphasizes observation, humility, moderation, and acceptance of human limits. Both resist vanity and recognize that the self is more complicated than public appearances suggest. Their examples do not promise perfection. They show that character can become more stable when people learn to examine their motives, respond to weakness realistically, and direct their abilities toward worthwhile relationships and purposes.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the two models and identify one practice from each that could improve your own self-examination."
Key point:
- Self-knowledge is a continuing practice rather than a final achievement.
- Truth, effort, humility, and compassion can help turn fragmentation into greater coherence.

Table 10
The Big Me: Authenticity, Meritocracy, Humility, and the Formation of Character
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 10, “The Big Me,” and how does it complete the book’s account of character?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that modern culture has strengthened the outward-facing self concerned with achievement, recognition, autonomy, and personal branding while weakening the inward-facing self concerned with humility, conscience, love, vocation, and moral struggle. Brooks does not say that confidence, self-expression, or ambition are inherently wrong. He argues that they become damaging when they displace the disciplines and relationships through which character is formed. The chapter therefore gathers the book’s earlier biographies into a diagnosis of the surrounding moral culture and a proposed Humility Code. Its central task is to restore proportion: develop competence and agency, but orient them toward a life of moral purpose and dependable attachment.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between outward achievement and inward formation, then identify one example of each from the chapter."
Key point:
- A successful life requires more than external accomplishment.
- Character depends on what a person becomes, not only what a person achieves.
2. Q: Why does the chapter contrast Johnny Unitas with Joe Namath?
A: Show answer
Unitas and Namath represent two different public styles of professional identity. The chapter portrays Unitas as understated, team-oriented, loyal, and focused on the craft of football, while Namath is presented as flamboyant, self-promoting, commercially visible, and comfortable making himself a celebrity. Their contrast illustrates a wider cultural movement from self-effacement toward self-expression and personal branding. It is not a claim that one athlete was morally perfect or that the other lacked talent. The useful distinction is between allowing a role and team to define public conduct and making the individual personality the central product. The Pro Football Hall of Fame independently records Unitas’s sustained professional achievements, including his career with the Colts and his induction in 1979.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the two athletes across public image, relationship to the team, and source of professional identity."
Key point:
- The athletes illustrate contrasting cultural styles rather than a simple good-versus-bad judgement.
- The chapter uses sports figures to introduce a broader change in moral culture.
